# SDM INTAKE POLICY, PROTOCOL, AND GUIDANCE

## Purpose

In North Carolina, any person who has cause to suspect a child is being maltreated (abused or neglected, or is dependent), is required by law to report their concerns to a local county child welfare agency. Child Protective Services Structured Decision Making (SDM) Screening and Response Tool is the first stage of the child welfare process and involves screening child protective services reports to determine what further action is required. Careful, detailed, and thorough work at CPS Intake lays the foundation for making well-informed decisions throughout the life of the case. The quality and consistency of the information gathered at CPS Intake directly impacts the safety of the alleged victim children and any subsequent intervention.

The goal of CPS Intake is to make consistent screening decisions using a structured intake process based on specific criteria. The steps of CPS Intake are:

1. Complete a new CPS Intake in the Partnership and Technology Hub North Carolina (PATH NC).
2. Consult the Maltreatment Screening Tool(s) that correspond to the allegations.
3. Determine the county responsible for completing the CPS Assessment.
4. Determine the appropriate Assessment Response Track (Investigative or Family) and Response Time (Immediate—8 hours, 24 hours, or

72 hours). Following the steps above, the CPS Intake process determines whether the reported information meets the statutory guidelines for child maltreatment. When a report is screened in (i.e., it meets the statutory requirements), CPS Intake determines the county responsible for the assessment, the response track, the response time, and then the report is assigned for CPS Assessment.

|The screening process requires<br>knowledge of the statutory definitions<br>of child abuse, neglect, dependency,<br>and caretaker. The local county child<br>welfare agency has the authority to<br>intervene only when the allegation, if<br>true, would meet the legal definitions.<br>Reports accepted for CPS Assessment<br>must clearly invoke the statutory<br>authority to provide Child Protective<br>Services.|The screening process requires<br>knowledge of the statutory definitions<br>of child abuse, neglect, dependency,<br>and caretaker. These services will help the<br>parents, guardians, other caretakers, and the court to prevent abuse or neglect; to improve the quality of<br>childcare; to be more adequate parents, guardians, or caretakers; and to preserve and stabilize family life.<br>N.C.G.S. §7B-301, Duty to report abuse, neglect, dependency, or death due to maltreatment:<br>(a) Any person or institution who has cause to suspect that any juvenile is abused, neglected, or dependent,<br>as defined N.C.G.S. §7B-101, or has died as the result of maltreatment, shall report the case of that<br>juvenile to the director of the department of social services in the county where the juvenile resides or is<br>found. The report may be made orally, by telephone, or in writing. The report shall include information<br>as is known to the person making it including: the name and address of the juvenile; the name and address<br>of the juvenile's parent, guardian, or caretaker; the age of the juvenile; the names and ages of other<br>juveniles in the home; the present whereabouts of the juvenile if not at the home address; the nature<br>and extent of any injury or condition resulting from abuse, neglect, or dependency; and any other<br>information which the person making the report believes might be helpful in establishing the need for<br>protective services or court intervention. If the report is made orally or by telephone, the person making<br>the report shall give the person's name, address, and telephone number. Refusal of the person making<br>the report to give a name shall not preclude the department's assessment of the alleged abuse, neglect,<br>dependency, or death because of maltreatment.<br>(b) Any person or institution who knowingly or wantonly fails to report the case of a juvenile as required by<br>subsection (a) of this section, or who knowingly or wantonly prevents another person from making a<br>report as required by subsection (a) of this section, is guilty of a Class 1 misdemeanor.<br>N.C.G.S. §7B 302(b) states: "When a report of a juvenile's death as a result of suspected maltreatment or a<br>report of suspected abuse, neglect, or dependency of a juvenile in a non-institutional setting is received the<br>director of the department of social services shall immediately ascertain if other juveniles remain in the<br>home, and, if so, initiate an assessment in order to determine whether they require protective services or<br>whether immediate removal of the juveniles from the home is necessary for their protection."|Protective services shall include the screening of reports; the performance of an assessment using either a<br>Family Assessment response or an Investigative Assessment response; casework; or other counseling<br>services to parents, guardians, or other caretakers as provided by the director. # Policy/Legal Basis

||||N.C.G.S. §7B-309, Immunity of persons reporting and cooperating in an assessment.<br>Anyone who makes a report pursuant to this Article; cooperates with the county department of social services<br>in a protective services assessment; testifies in any judicial proceeding resulting from a protective services<br>report or assessment; provides information or assistance, including medical evaluations or consultation in<br>connection with a report, investigation, or legal intervention pursuant to a good-faith report of child abuse<br>or neglect: or otherwise participates in the program authorized by this Article; is immune from any civil or<br>criminal liability that might otherwise be incurred or imposed for that action provided that the person was<br>acting in good faith.<br>Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (CAPTA) requires health care providers involved in the delivery<br>and care of infants born with and identified as being affected by substance abuse, withdrawal symptoms<br>resulting from prenatal drug exposure, or a Fetal Alcohol Spectrum Disorder to notify the child protective<br>services system of the occurrence.<br>"[T]he clear and convincing evidence in the record must show current circumstances that present a risk to<br>the juvenile."_In re J.A.M._, 372 N.C. 1, 9, 822 S.E.2d 693, 698 (2019). But "[t]he trial court is granted some<br>discretion in determining whether children are at risk for a particular kind of harm given their age and the<br>environment in which they reside."_In re A.D._, 278 N.C. App. 637, 642, 863 S.E.2d 317, 321-22 (2021)<br>(internal quotations and citation omitted). "It is well established that the trial court need not wait for<br>actual harm to occur to the child if there is a substantial risk of harm to the child in the home."_In re D.B.J._, <br>197 N.C. App. 752, 755, 678 S.E.2d 778, 780 (2009).<br> Matter of K.C., 295 N.C. App. 363, 369, 905 S.E.2d 776, 781 (2024)<br>Before adjudicating a juvenile neglected, the trial court must also find "some physical, mental, or<br>emotional impairment of the juvenile or a substantial risk of such impairment as a consequence of the<br>failure to provide 'proper care, supervision, or discipline.' "_In re Stumbo_, 357 N.C. 279, 283, 582 S.E.2d<br>255, 258 (2003) (quoting_In re Safriet_, 112 N.C. App. 747, 752, 436 S.E.2d 898, 901-02 (1993)). With<br>newborns, "the decision of the trial court must of necessity be predictive in nature, as the trial court must<br>assess whether there is a substantial risk of future abuse or neglect of a child based on the historical facts<br>of the case."_In re McLean_, 135 N.C. App. 387, 396, 521 S.E.2d 121, 127(1999). With<br>newborns, "the decision of the trial court must of necessity be predictive in nature, as the trial court must<br>assess whether there is a substantial risk of future abuse or neglect of a child based on the historical facts<br>of the case."_In re McLean_, 135 N.C. App. 387, 396, 521 S.E.2d 121, 127(1999). The Supreme Court of|N.C.G.S. §7B-309, Immunity of persons reporting and cooperating in an assessment.<br>Anyone who makes a report pursuant to this Article; cooperates with the county department of social services<br>in a protective services assessment; testifies in any judicial proceeding resulting from a protective services<br>report or assessment; provides information or assistance, including medical evaluations or consultation in<br>connection with a report, investigation, or legal intervention pursuant to a good-faith report of child abuse<br>or neglect: or otherwise participates in the program authorized by this Article; is immune from any civil or<br>criminal liability that might otherwise be incurred or imposed for that action provided that the person was<br>acting in good faith.<br>Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (CAPTA) requires health care providers involved in the delivery<br>and care of infants born with and identified as being affected by substance abuse, withdrawal symptoms<br>resulting from prenatal drug exposure, or a Fetal Alcohol Spectrum Disorder to notify the child protective<br>services system of the occurrence.<br>"[T]he clear and convincing evidence in the record must show current circumstances that present a risk to<br>the juvenile."_In re J.A.M._, 372 N.C. 1, 9, 822 S.E.2d 693, 698 (2019). But "[t]he trial court is granted some<br>discretion in determining whether children are at risk for a particular kind of harm given their age and the<br>environment in which they reside."_In re A.D._, 278 N.C. App. 637, 642, 863 S.E.2d 317, 321-22 (2021)<br>(internal quotations and citation omitted). "It is well established that the trial court need not wait for<br>actual harm to occur to the child if there is a substantial risk of harm to the child in the home."_In re D.B.J._, <br>197 N.C. App. 752, 755, 678 S.E.2d 778, 780 (2009).<br> Matter of K.C., 295 N.C. App. 363, 369, 905 S.E.2d 776, 781 (2024)<br>Before adjudicating a juvenile neglected, the trial court must also find "some physical, mental, or<br>emotional impairment of the juvenile or a substantial risk of such impairment as a consequence of the<br>failure to provide 'proper care, supervision, or discipline.' "_In re Stumbo_, 357 N.C. 279, 283, 582 S.E.2d<br>255, 258 (2003) (quoting_In re Safriet_, 112 N.C. App. 747, 752, 436 S.E.2d 898, 901-02 (1993)). With<br>newborns, "the decision of the trial court must of necessity be predictive in nature, as the trial court must<br>assess whether there is a substantial risk of future abuse or neglect of a child based on the historical facts<br>of the case."_In re McLean_, 135 N.C. App. 387, 396, 521 S.E.2d 121, 127(1999). The Supreme Court of|

# Policy/Legal Basis

| | Policy | | | Legal Basis | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
||||North Carolina has found neglect in cases where "the conduct at issue constituted either severe or<br>dangerous conduct or a pattern of conduct either causing injury or potentially causing injury to the<br>juvenile."_In re Stumbo_, 357 N.C. at 283, 582 S.E.2d at 258.<br> Matter of K.C., 295 N.C. App. 363, 368-69, 905 S.E.2d 776, 781 (2024)|North Carolina has found neglect in cases where "the conduct at issue constituted either severe or<br>dangerous conduct or a pattern of conduct either causing injury or potentially causing injury to the<br>juvenile."_In re Stumbo_, 357 N.C. at 283, 582 S.E.2d at 258.<br> Matter of K.C., 295 N.C. |(e) Creates or allows to be created serious emotional damage to the juvenile. Serious emotional damage is evidenced by a juvenile's severe<br>anxiety, depression, withdrawal, or aggressive behavior toward himself or others;<br>(f) Encourages, directs, or approves of delinquent acts involving moral turpitude committed by the juvenile; or<br>(g) Commits or allows to be committed an offense under N.C.G.S. §14-43.11 (human trafficking), N.C.G.S. §14-43.12 (involuntary servitude), or<br>N.C.G.S. §14-43.13 (sexual servitude) against the child.<br>Neglected Juvenile: Any juvenile less than 18 years of age (i) who is found to be a minor victim of human trafficking under G.S. 14-43.15 or (ii) whose<br>parent, guardian, custodian or caretaker does not provide any of the following:<br>(a) does not provide proper care, supervision, or discipline.<br>(b) Has abandoned the juvenile.<br>(c) Has not provided or arranged for the provision of necessary medical or remedial care<br>(d) or whose parent, guardian, or custodian has refused to follow the recommendations of the Juvenile and Family Team made pursuant to<br>Chapter 7B Article 27A.<br>(e) Creates or allows to be created a living environment that is injurious to the juvenile's welfare.<br>(f) Has participated or attempted to participate in the unlawful transfer of custody of the juvenile underG.S. 14-321.2<br>In determining whether a juvenile is a neglected juvenile, it is relevant whether that juvenile lives in a home where another juvenile has died as a<br>result of suspected abuse or neglect or lives in a home where another juvenile has been subjected to abuse by an adult who regularly lives in the<br>home. Relevance in this instance is a legal term that means that the court can find a child neglected on the basis that he or she was living with a victim<br>child.<br>Dependent Juvenile: A juvenile in need of assistance or placement because the juvenile has no parent, guardian, or custodian responsible for the<br>juvenile's care or supervision or whose parent, guardian, or custodian, is unable to provide for the care or supervision and lacks an appropriate<br>alternative childcare arrangement.|(e) Creates or allows to be created serious emotional damage to the juvenile. # Anonymous Reporters/Reporter Confidentiality

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|North Carolina legislation requires that the person making the report give<br>their name, address, and telephone number. However, refusal of the person<br>making the report to identify himself or herself does not relieve the local<br>county child welfare agency of its responsibility for conducting a CPS<br>Assessment if the allegations meet the statutory definition for acceptance.<br>North Carolina statute requires:<br>•<br>The identity of the person making the report, and<br>•<br>All information obtained during a CPS Assessment must be held in<br>the strictest confidence by the local county child welfare agency.<br>This statute does not grant the right of the reporter to remain anonymous.<br>However, the reporter must be informed that their identity will remain<br>confidential unless:<br>•<br>A court orders otherwise;<br>•<br>A local, state, or federal entity demonstrates a need for the<br>reporter's name to carry out its mandated responsibilities.<br>Statute does not require that the reporter to possess any information<br>beyond a cause to suspect abuse or neglect. The reporter is not required to<br>have witnessed the abuse or neglect or to have firsthand knowledge.|North Carolina legislation requires that the person making the report give<br>their name, address, and telephone number. The reporter is not required to<br>have witnessed the abuse or neglect or to have firsthand knowledge.|Local County child welfare agencies often need to speak with a<br>reporter more than once to clarify or follow up on other issues, so<br>anonymous calls should be discouraged as much as possible. If a local<br>county child welfare agency has "Caller Identification," the staff should<br>make any caller aware that the agency has this information, especially<br>if the identifying information the caller is giving is different from the<br>information on the "Caller Identification." If the local county child<br>welfare agency knows the identity of the reporter, that information<br>should be recorded on the PATH NC Screening and Response Tool<br>under the information for Reporter, even if the caller wishes to remain<br>anonymous. If the local county child<br>welfare agency knows the identity of the reporter, that information<br>should be recorded on the PATH NC Screening and Response Tool<br>under the information for Reporter, even if the caller wishes to remain<br>anonymous. The Intake caseworker should include that the reporter<br>would like to remain anonymous at the top of the narrative<br>information box and mark the role of the reporter as "Individual Not<br>Provided" or INP.|

# Collection of Information and Assessing Agency History

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**CREATING A NEW CPS INTAKE**<br>The Intake caseworker must document and manage information<br>about the report of suspected abuse, neglect, and/or dependency<br>by creating a new CPS Intake in the PATH NC Screening and<br>Response Tool. A new CPS Intake begins with the collection of<br>reporter information.<br>RESPONSE TIME<br>Response times include responding to and fully initiating<br>screened in reports of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency. The<br>timeframe to complete an initiation begins at the time the<br>reporter contacts the local county child welfare agency. A<br>report is not considered initiated until all victim children have<br>been interviewed thoroughly regarding the allegations. Local<br>county child welfare agencies must provide Child Protective<br>Services, including Intake, 24 hours per day, 7 days per week,<br>and begin the initiation process immediately in emergency<br>situations when identified in the Screening and Response Tool.<br>The Intake caseworker must provide support and encouragement<br>to the reporter by:<br>● Explaining the purpose of CPS (to ensure safety of children,<br>and protect and strengthen the family);<br>● Emphasizing the importance of reporting;<br>● Dealing with the fears and concerns of the reporter; and<br>● Discussing confidentiality regarding the CPS report<br>including the identity of the reporter.|**CREATING A NEW CPS INTAKE**<br>The Intake caseworker must document and manage information<br>about the report of suspected abuse, neglect, and/or dependency<br>by creating a new CPS Intake in the PATH NC Screening and<br>Response Tool. A<br>report is not considered initiated until all victim children have<br>been interviewed thoroughly regarding the allegations. Local<br>county child welfare agencies must provide Child Protective<br>Services, including Intake, 24 hours per day, 7 days per week,<br>and begin the initiation process immediately in emergency<br>situations when identified in the Screening and Response Tool.<br>The Intake caseworker must provide support and encouragement<br>to the reporter by:<br>● Explaining the purpose of CPS (to ensure safety of children,<br>and protect and strengthen the family);<br>● Emphasizing the importance of reporting;<br>● Dealing with the fears and concerns of the reporter; and<br>● Discussing confidentiality regarding the CPS report<br>including the identity of the reporter.|**CREATING A NEW CPS INTAKE**<br>The Intake caseworker must document and manage information<br>about the report of suspected abuse, neglect, and/or dependency<br>by creating a new CPS Intake in the PATH NC Screening and<br>Response Tool. A new CPS Intake begins with the collection of<br>reporter information.<br>RESPONSE TIME<br>Response times include responding to and fully initiating<br>screened in reports of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency. The<br>timeframe to complete an initiation begins at the time the<br>reporter contacts the local county child welfare agency. A<br>report is not considered initiated until all victim children have<br>been interviewed thoroughly regarding the allegations. Local<br>county child welfare agencies must provide Child Protective<br>Services, including Intake, 24 hours per day, 7 days per week,<br>and begin the initiation process immediately in emergency<br>situations when identified in the Screening and Response Tool.<br>The Intake caseworker must provide support and encouragement<br>to the reporter by:<br>● Explaining the purpose of CPS (to ensure safety of children,<br>and protect and strengthen the family);<br>● Emphasizing the importance of reporting;<br>● Dealing with the fears and concerns of the reporter; and<br>● Discussing confidentiality regarding the CPS report<br>including the identity of the reporter.|Making effective CPS Intake decisions requires Intake caseworkers to have<br>competent interviewing skills, awareness of the information needed, the skill to<br>organize and analyze information, to arrive at accurate conclusions, and the ability<br>to be supportive of reporters. The reporter's information regarding the allegations<br>needs to be listened to and documented on the PATH NC Screening and Response<br>Tool, and to ensure that Intake has collected all the information provided by the<br>reporter. The Intake caseworker should access the Desk Guide for additional<br>questions to assist them in completing a comprehensive Intake assessment.<br>Every effort should be made to speak with the reporter at the time the call is<br>placed. In instances when the reporter left a message, the timeframe for response<br>begins at the time the reporter left the message, not the time the call was<br>returned. There may be times when the reporter does not have access to a<br>telephone on a regular basis and cannot leave a number for the call to be<br>returned. Assessing safety and if an immediate response is required is impossible<br>when those concerns are left on a messaging system.<br>In cases where the reporter has concerns about making a report, the Intake<br>caseworker can point them to NCGS § 7B-301 that requires any person over the<br>age of 18 that suspectsabuse, neglect, or dependency of a juvenile to make a<br>report to the local county child welfare agency. This suspicion does not require the<br>reporter to have witnessed or have firsthand knowledge of the allegations. This<br>statute also assures immunity from civil and criminal liability as long as the report<br>was made in good faith. The reporter must be supported in their decision to make<br>a report and address their concerns. # Collection of Information and Assessing Agency History

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|MANDATORY QUESTIONS<br>The Intake caseworker must answer a series of mandatory<br>questions in the Screening and Response Tool prior to gathering<br>the narrative information to assist with determining whether the<br>use of the Screening and Response Tool is necessary. If the<br>allegations are not regarding a child and there are no other<br>children in the home, the Intake caseworker will bypass the<br>remainder of the tool and screen out the report. In cases involving<br>allegations of abuse or severe neglect against an individual who is<br>not a caretaker, with the exception of Human Trafficking, the<br>Intake caseworker will still gather the narrative and participant<br>information from the reporter and send it to the appropriate<br>agency.<br>In cases where the agency does not have statutory authority to<br>intervene because there is not a child under 18 in the home or the<br>allegations do not involve a caretaker, except Human Trafficking,<br>the information will be screened out prior to completion of the<br>Screening and Response Tool and go straight to the supervisor for<br>second level review. The Mandatory Questions include:<br>1. Is the victim child under 18 years of age?<br>2. Is the child a victim of fatality?<br>3. The person(s) with firsthand knowledge should be listed in the<br>collateral section of the Screening and Response Tool.<br>MANDATORY QUESTIONS<br>It will be important for Intake caseworkers to be able to communicate the<br>definitions for words such as caretaker, Human Trafficking, and Domestic Violence<br>that may not be familiar to the reporter so they can understand and answer the<br>questions accurately.<br>The Intake system has built-in decision-making assistance by responding to<br>questions in an if/then format. In the case of Mandatory Questions, it provides<br>assistance with pre-screening. If the victim is over 18 the system will provide a<br>notification about referral of the information to law enforcement or Adult<br>Protective Services (APS). It will also ask if there are any other children under 18 in<br>the home and if the answer is no, the system will bypass the Screening and<br>Response Tool and send to the supervisor for review. If the child is the victim of a<br>fatality and there are no other children in the home, the system will bypass the<br>Screening and Response Tool and send to the supervisor for review. There will be a<br>secondary question to ensure that the fatality is reported to law enforcement. CPS<br>does not have jurisdiction in cases where there are no children under 18 years of<br>age. In cases that do not involve youth under 18 years of age, the Intake<br>caseworker should direct notification to the appropriate entity such as law<br>enforcement or Adult Protective Services (APS).<br>Most questions are in a yes/no format. The|"Near fatality" is a phrase used in child protective services and is defined as<br>follows:<br>•<br>An act that, as certified by a physician, places a child in serious or critical<br>condition (Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (CAPTA), Chapter 67,<br>subchapter 1)<br>•<br>NC G.S. § 7B-2902.9(a)(3) defines a Near fatality as "A case in which a<br>physician determines that a child is in serious or critical condition as the<br>result of sickness or injury caused by suspected abuse, neglect, or<br>maltreatment."<br>Determination of a condition by a physician (serious or critical) can be made<br>verbally and/or in writing. The level and extent of medical<br>intervention may be an indicator in this distinction.<br>The condition of the child admitted to the ICU must be considered. When an adult<br>parent/caretaker remains "unknown" the assessment case head is required to<br>follow the guidelines listed in the protocol section and can be found as a help text<br>in the system. A case head has to be documented before you move on to the next<br>section of the Screening and Response Tool. Any additional information obtained<br>during the Assessment should not be updated in the Screening and Response tool<br>to ensure that the reporter does not get information that they did not provide to<br>the agency and maintain the family's confidentiality.<br>Only living participants should be added to the participant information except in<br>cases with a child fatality. Each deceased child should be added as a participant<br>and the box indicating a fatality is required to be checked. All other deceased<br>participants, including parents or caretakers, should be documented in the<br>Narrative Information box. The system will not allow the Intake caseworker to add<br>maltreatments to the deceased child.<br>When a case involves an infant that has been safely surrendered to the local<br>county child welfare agency, the agency should include all information provided by<br>the reporter. This includes the parents (surrendering and non-surrendering), if<br>known, along with any medical/health information about the family that can be<br>obtained. This information must be kept confidential and can only be shared in<br>accordance with NCGS § 7B-524.|COLLECT PARTICIPANT INFORMATION<br>A case head can be marked as "unknown" during intake and can change during the<br>Assessment once that information becomes known. When an adult<br>parent/caretaker remains "unknown" the assessment case head is required to<br>follow the guidelines listed in the protocol section and can be found as a help text<br>in the system. A case head has to be documented before you move on to the next<br>section of the Screening and Response Tool. Any additional information obtained<br>during the Assessment should not be updated in the Screening and Response tool<br>to ensure that the reporter does not get information that they did not provide to<br>the agency and maintain the family's confidentiality.<br>Only living participants should be added to the participant information except in<br>cases with a child fatality. Each deceased child should be added as a participant<br>and the box indicating a fatality is required to be checked. All other deceased<br>participants, including parents or caretakers, should be documented in the<br>Narrative Information box. # Collection of Information and Assessing Agency History

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|If the parent or caretaker is unknown, the case head is identified by<br>the child in the assessment. If there is more than one child, the<br>case head is that of the youngest child.<br>IDENTIFYING RELATIVES AND KIN<br>Beginning at Intake, the identification of relatives and kin, including<br>non-relative/fictive kin, can help to promote the safety of children.<br>This information should be included in the mandatory question<br>section under the question, "Are you aware of any relatives or kin<br>supports for this family? Asking about relatives and kin includes<br>those who live outside of the home but can provide support to the<br>family.<br>Collection of Information and Assessing Agency History<br>The Intake caseworker must gather sufficient information from the<br>reporter to be able to:<br>● Identify and locate case participants (child(ren), parents, or<br>primary caretaker) and;<br>● Determine if the report meets the statutory guidelines for<br>child maltreatment;<br>● Assess the seriousness of the child's situation; and<br>● Understand the relationship of the reporter to the family<br>and the motives of the reporter.<br>● Identify collaterals who may have additional information<br>for the assessment (medical/behavioral health care<br>providers, teachers, friends, family, or others) and their<br>relationship to the alleged victim child(ren).<br>The Intake caseworker must check the service history and/or<br>county agency records to determine if the caseparticipants(family|If the parent or caretaker is unknown, the case head is identified by<br>the child in the assessment. # Substance Affected Infants

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (CAPTA) requires that all<br>infants identified as "substance affected" by a healthcare provider,<br>receive a Plan of Safe Care. When a local county child welfare<br>agency receives notification of the birth and identification of a<br>substance affected infant, it must begin the process of ensuring a<br>Plan of Safe Care is identified and developed for each infant by:<br>● Completing a Care Management for At Risk Children<br>(CMARC) referral formand<br>● Submitting the referral form to the local CMARC program<br>or designated alternative service.<br>To comply with confidentiality laws and to ensure that a Plan of Safe<br>Care can be created for every infant, it is important that the CMARC<br>referral be made during the screening of the report and prior to<br>deciding to the screening decision during the 2nd level review.<br>Once the referral has been made, the local county child welfare<br>agency must follow the guidance contained in the Child Welfare<br>Resources for Substance Affected Infants and Plan of Safe Careas<br>well as consult the Substance Affected Infant Maltreatment<br>Definition to determine if a CPS Assessment is warranted.|Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (CAPTA) requires that all<br>infants identified as "substance affected" by a healthcare provider,<br>receive a Plan of Safe Care. When a local county child welfare<br>agency receives notification of the birth and identification of a<br>substance affected infant, it must begin the process of ensuring a<br>Plan of Safe Care is identified and developed for each infant by:<br>● Completing a Care Management for At Risk Children<br>(CMARC) referral formand<br>● Submitting the referral form to the local CMARC program<br>or designated alternative service.<br>To comply with confidentiality laws and to ensure that a Plan of Safe<br>Care can be created for every infant, it is important that the CMARC<br>referral be made during the screening of the report and prior to<br>deciding to the screening decision during the 2nd level review.<br>Once the referral has been made, the local county child welfare<br>agency must follow the guidance contained in the Child Welfare<br>Resources for Substance Affected Infants and Plan of Safe Careas<br>well as consult the Substance Affected Infant Maltreatment<br>Definition to determine if a CPS Assessment is warranted.|Child Abuse Prevention and Treatment Act (CAPTA) requires that all<br>infants identified as "substance affected" by a healthcare provider,<br>receive a Plan of Safe Care. When a local county child welfare<br>agency receives notification of the birth and identification of a<br>substance affected infant, it must begin the process of ensuring a<br>Plan of Safe Care is identified and developed for each infant by:<br>● Completing a Care Management for At Risk Children<br>(CMARC) referral formand<br>● Submitting the referral form to the local CMARC program<br>or designated alternative service.<br>To comply with confidentiality laws and to ensure that a Plan of Safe<br>Care can be created for every infant, it is important that the CMARC<br>referral be made during the screening of the report and prior to<br>deciding to the screening decision during the 2nd level review.<br>Once the referral has been made, the local county child welfare<br>agency must follow the guidance contained in the Child Welfare<br>Resources for Substance Affected Infants and Plan of Safe Careas<br>well as consult the Substance Affected Infant Maltreatment<br>Definition to determine if a CPS Assessment is warranted.|The timing of the referral is critical because confidentiality laws prohibit a local<br>county child welfare agency from making the referral to CMARC if the report<br>has already been screened out and child protective services are no longer<br>being provided.<br>Intake and Screening of the Report<br>Healthcare providers have to make notification of all infants born with signs of<br>withdrawal symptoms due to prenatal drug exposure or diagnosed with Fetal<br>Alcohol Spectrum Disorder (FASD) even if the situation does not necessarily<br>constitute child abuse or neglect. A report that only alleges that an infant was<br>exposed to substances prior to birth does not intrinsically meet the statutory<br>definition of child abuse, neglect, or dependency. It is the responsibility of the<br>Intake caseworker to gather enough information regarding the impact of the<br>parents' substance use on the infant to determine whether the notification<br>meets the threshold to screen in a report.<br>Current standard practice dictates that any information the local county child<br>welfare agency obtains that is protected by federal regulations should not be<br>disclosed absent a court order or proper client consent. Additionally, the name<br>of the reporter must remain confidential. See OBTAINING SUBSTANCE ABUSE<br>RECORDS BY COURT ORDER in the Juvenile "Court and Child Welfare" section<br>of Cross Function Topics in the NC Child Welfare manualfor more information<br>on 42 C.F.R. Part 2 regulations.|The timing of the referral is critical because confidentiality laws prohibit a local<br>county child welfare agency from making the referral to CMARC if the report<br>has already been screened out and child protective services are no longer<br>being provided.<br>Intake and Screening of the Report<br>Healthcare providers have to make notification of all infants born with signs of<br>withdrawal symptoms due to prenatal drug exposure or diagnosed with Fetal<br>Alcohol Spectrum Disorder (FASD) even if the situation does not necessarily<br>constitute child abuse or neglect. A report that only alleges that an infant was<br>exposed to substances prior to birth does not intrinsically meet the statutory<br>definition of child abuse, neglect, or dependency. It is the responsibility of the<br>Intake caseworker to gather enough information regarding the impact of the<br>parents' substance use on the infant to determine whether the notification<br>meets the threshold to screen in a report.<br>Current standard practice dictates that any information the local county child<br>welfare agency obtains that is protected by federal regulations should not be<br>disclosed absent a court order or proper client consent. Part 2 regulations.|The timing of the referral is critical because confidentiality laws prohibit a local<br>county child welfare agency from making the referral to CMARC if the report<br>has already been screened out and child protective services are no longer<br>being provided.<br>Intake and Screening of the Report<br>Healthcare providers have to make notification of all infants born with signs of<br>withdrawal symptoms due to prenatal drug exposure or diagnosed with Fetal<br>Alcohol Spectrum Disorder (FASD) even if the situation does not necessarily<br>constitute child abuse or neglect. A report that only alleges that an infant was<br>exposed to substances prior to birth does not intrinsically meet the statutory<br>definition of child abuse, neglect, or dependency. <!-- page: 19 -->
# Human Trafficking

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|Human Trafficking must be selected in the pre-screening Mandatory Questions<br>if the reporter identifies concerns or the information provided causes the Intake<br>caseworker to suspect that there is a concern. The Human Trafficking<br>Maltreatment Allegation located in the Screening and Response Tool must be<br>selected when:<br>•<br>A reporter indicates or suspects trafficking<br>•<br>The CPS Intake caseworker suspects trafficking based on the<br>maltreatment allegations<br>The narrative information must include the child's parent, guardian, custodian,<br>or caretaker information:<br>•<br>Protective capacity;<br>•<br>Willingness to take protective action; and<br>•<br>Role in the trafficking.<br>Human Trafficking is a child who is sold, traded, or exchanged for anything, or<br>to settle a debt, regardless of whether the child is used for labor or sex.<br>For additional information about human trafficking, see Cross Function Topic:<br>Human Trafficking.|Human Trafficking must be selected in the pre-screening Mandatory Questions<br>if the reporter identifies concerns or the information provided causes the Intake<br>caseworker to suspect that there is a concern. The Human Trafficking<br>Maltreatment Allegation located in the Screening and Response Tool must be<br>selected when:<br>•<br>A reporter indicates or suspects trafficking<br>•<br>The CPS Intake caseworker suspects trafficking based on the<br>maltreatment allegations<br>The narrative information must include the child's parent, guardian, custodian,<br>or caretaker information:<br>•<br>Protective capacity;<br>•<br>Willingness to take protective action; and<br>•<br>Role in the trafficking.<br>Human Trafficking is a child who is sold, traded, or exchanged for anything, or<br>to settle a debt, regardless of whether the child is used for labor or sex.<br>For additional information about human trafficking, see Cross Function Topic:<br>Human Trafficking.|Human Trafficking must be selected in the pre-screening Mandatory Questions<br>if the reporter identifies concerns or the information provided causes the Intake<br>caseworker to suspect that there is a concern. The Human Trafficking<br>Maltreatment Allegation located in the Screening and Response Tool must be<br>selected when:<br>•<br>A reporter indicates or suspects trafficking<br>•<br>The CPS Intake caseworker suspects trafficking based on the<br>maltreatment allegations<br>The narrative information must include the child's parent, guardian, custodian,<br>or caretaker information:<br>•<br>Protective capacity;<br>•<br>Willingness to take protective action; and<br>•<br>Role in the trafficking.<br>Human Trafficking is a child who is sold, traded, or exchanged for anything, or<br>to settle a debt, regardless of whether the child is used for labor or sex.<br>For additional information about human trafficking, see Cross Function Topic:<br>Human Trafficking.|When asking a reporter about Human Trafficking, it may not be<br>enough to simply ask the question. You may need to provide the<br>definition and additional clarifying questions to help them answer<br>the question.<br>Perpetrators of trafficking may be family members or may pose as<br>the child or youth's parent, sibling, aunt, uncle, or significant other<br>and their relationship to the child may be unclear - even to the<br>reporter.<br>Often persons who exploit children and youth through sex or labor<br>trafficking make promises to children or to families that the child<br>will have improved circumstances, access to education, or a job if<br>the child moves from one place to another. Sometimes this involves<br>moving from another country.<br>If a child has moved from another country to the United States<br>without a family member or is traveling with an adult to whom they<br>are not related or with whom the relationship is unclear, it is<br>possible that the child is being trafficked or is at risk of being<br>trafficked. Intake caseworkers should gather as much information<br>as the reporter is able to provide concerning the child and the<br>child's circumstances, including:<br>•<br>Where they are traveling from;<br>•<br>Where they are traveling to;<br>•<br>Who (if anyone) they are traveling with and their<br>relationship to this person;<br>•<br>The reason for coming to the United States, as provided by<br>the child, the person with whom they are traveling or with<br>whom they reside; and|When asking a reporter about Human Trafficking, it may not be<br>enough to simply ask the question. You may need to provide the<br>definition and additional clarifying questions to help them answer<br>the question.<br>Perpetrators of trafficking may be family members or may pose as<br>the child or youth's parent, sibling, aunt, uncle, or significant other<br>and their relationship to the child may be unclear - even to the<br>reporter.<br>Often persons who exploit children and youth through sex or labor<br>trafficking make promises to children or to families that the child<br>will have improved circumstances, access to education, or a job if<br>the child moves from one place to another. |**JUVENILE AND CARETAKER DETERMINATION**<br>**Determine Juvenile Involvement**<br>Any reports of maltreatment that do not involve a juvenile will be automatically<br>screened out and sent to a supervisor for a 2nd level review. See definitions.<br>However, all children living in the home of the victim child identified in the report<br>must be considered alleged victim children.<br>**Does the Report Meet Abuse, Neglect, &/or Dependency Definitions?**<br>Local county child welfare agencies only have the authority to intervene when the<br>allegations, if true, meet the statutory definitions of abuse, neglect, and/or<br>dependency. See definitions.<br>**Is the Alleged Perpetrator a Parent, Guardian, Custodian or Caretaker?**<br>Reports that do not involve the maltreatment of a child by a parent, guardian,<br>custodian or caretaker will be automatically screened out and sent to a supervisor<br>for 2nd level review, exceptfor those alleging human trafficking.<br>**Caretaker**<br>Local county child welfare agencies must screen reports to determine whether the<br>alleged perpetrator meets the statutory definition of a caretaker at the time the<br>maltreatment occurred. |the child. The totality of the circumstances must be considered in these instances<br>including:<br>● The duration and frequency of care provided by the adult;<br>● The location in which that care is provided; and<br>● The decision-making authority granted to the adult.<br>Local county child welfare agencies must assess whether the parent made an<br>appropriate decision regarding the child's safety and welfare when he or she placed<br>the child with the relative/caretaker, regardless of whether the alleged perpetrator<br>meets the caretaker definition. When allegations involve an adult who does not<br>meet the caretaker definition, but provides care for the child, the parent, custodian,<br>or guardian's decision to allow the adult to provide care and their response to the<br>abuse or neglect must be screened for maltreatment.<br>When a report of maltreatment involves a person that the reporter is unable to<br>identify as the caretaker the agency must mark "yes" in the system. The report<br>must be accepted for assessment if it cannot be determined whether the alleged<br>perpetrator meets the statutory definition of a caretaker.<br>While every "employee or volunteer" of a juvenile justice secure detention facility is<br>no longer subject to a CPS Assessment, the caretaker definition still applies to<br>employees who have "responsibility for the health and welfare of a juvenile" at<br>such a facility. See Out-of-Home Care Providers. <br>Reports alleging maltreatment in boarding schools are also subject to a CPS<br>Assessment based on the caretaker definition.<br>**Non-Caretaker Reports**<br>When a report of maltreatment is not accepted for CPS Assessment but includes|the child. The report<br>must be accepted for assessment if it cannot be determined whether the alleged<br>perpetrator meets the statutory definition of a caretaker.<br>While every "employee or volunteer" of a juvenile justice secure detention facility is<br>no longer subject to a CPS Assessment, the caretaker definition still applies to<br>employees who have "responsibility for the health and welfare of a juvenile" at<br>such a facility. # Out-of-Home Care Provider CPS Reports

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**REPORTS INVOLVING OPEN CHILD PLACEMENT CASES**<br>When a child is born to a parent with an open Foster Care case, prior to Termination or<br>relinquishment of their parental rights, a new report must be made to assess the safety of<br>a newborn in a household where other children have been removed.<br>G.S. 7B-101 (15) g. _In determining whether a juvenile is a neglected juvenile, it is relevant_<br>_whether that juvenile lives in a home where another juvenile has died as a result of_<br>_suspected abuse or neglect or lives in a home where another juvenile has been subjected_<br>_to abuse or neglect by an adult who regularly lives in the home._<br>These reports, absent any other maltreatment reported, must be assessed as a Family<br>Assessment, Physical Neglect—Unsafe Living Conditions. Response Time must be<br>determined based upon current circumstances of the open 109/Permanency Planning<br>case.<br>**REPORTS INVOLVING OUT-OF-HOME CARE PROVIDERS**<br>Reports that allege a child is being maltreated by an out-of-home care provider, and the<br>out-of-home provider meets the definition of caretaker per G.S. §7B-101, the local county<br>child welfare agency must complete a CPS Assessment using the**Investigative**<br>**Assessment**Track.<br>In cases where the report alleges that the child has been maltreated by a licensed foster<br>parent/group home, a notification must be made to NC DSS within 5 days to ensure<br>Licensing and Regulatory are aware. NC DSS licenses all foster parents, therapeutic foster<br>parent, and level 1 group homes. This notification must be made for ALL homes with a<br>foster care license regardless of whether there is currently a foster child in the home. # Multiple Reports Involving the Same Child or Family

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**MULTIPLE REPORTS INVOLVING THE SAME CHILD OR FAMILY**<br>If a CPS report is received that describes maltreatment allegations and the<br>incident is currently being assessed through an open CPS Assessment:<br>●<br>The information must be on the Screening and Response Tool in the<br>PATH NC System, screened-in, and merged with the current open<br>assessment.<br>●<br>The report may not require a new initiation; therefore, a<br>determination of the response timeframe is unnecessary.<br>A new CPS Intake must be created when new maltreatment allegations that<br>meet the legal definition of abuse, neglect, and or dependency are received<br>from the public while there is an open CPS Assessment (i.e., not uncovered<br>by the Intake caseworker as a part of the initiation in an open Assessment).<br>The new information is considered a new report and must be responded to<br>within time frames determined in the Screening and Response Tool. <!-- page: 36 -->
# Reports Creating a Conflict of Interest for the Agency

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|The family's (child's) county of residence is responsible for conducting the<br>CPS Assessment, except in cases where there is a Conflict of Interest (COI)<br>present. When there is a COI, another local county child welfare agency<br>must conduct the assessment.<br>The narrative information must be collected by the local county child welfare<br>agency that receives the report and then the report must be sent in the<br>PATH NC System to the local county child welfare agency conducting the CPS<br>Assessment for screening in the Screening and Response Tool. Verbal<br>confirmation must be obtained by the local county child welfare agency<br>sending the report for screening.<br>Transition into the PATH NC System: When a county that is in the PATH NC<br>System receives a report that is a COI the local county child welfare agency<br>must take the information from the reporter and put it in the PATH NC<br>System. If the county receiving the COI report is in PATH NC System, then the<br>information gathered must be sent in the system for screening and response<br>by the receiving county.<br>If the county receiving the COI is not in the PATH NC System, then<br>information gathered from the reporter must be printed out and immediate<br>notification made to the county receiving the report. The sending county<br>must ensure that it is received and then the receiving county must screen<br>the report and if accepted determine the assessment track and response<br>time based on the CPS Intake policy.<br>When a county that has not transitioned into the PATH NC System receives a<br>report that is a COI the local county child welfare must take the information<br>on the current DSS-1402, make immediate notification to the other county<br>regardless of whether they are in the PATH NC System or not. # Reports Creating a Conflict of Interest for the Agency

| response time determination. The county sending the information must<br>ensure that it is received.<br>If the county is in the PATH NC System the information must be entered into<br>the system and use the SDM Screening and Response Tool.<br>The county completing the conflict of interest must make a screening<br>decision and, if accepted, determine the assessment track and response<br>time UNLESS the information provided in the report is urgent and requires<br>an immediate response by the agency receiving the report. Verbal<br>notification must also be made informing the county of the child's location<br>and that initiation has begun by the county receiving the report.<br>See "Conflict of Interest" in the Cross Function manual in the NC Child<br>Welfare manual for further information regarding a local county child<br>welfare agency's responsibility when there is a conflict of interest. | |

# Intake Decision Making

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**SCREENING AND RESPONSE TOOL**<br>Use of the Screening and Response tool:<br>● Must occur for every CPS report:<br>`o` Regarding a child under the age of 18, and<br>`o` Alleging abuse, neglect, and/or dependency by a parent or caretaker, and/or<br>human trafficking<br>● Provides for accurate, consistent, and equitable screening across the state; and<br>● Determines whether the allegations meet the legal definitions of abuse, neglect<br>and/or dependency.<br>● Determines a Response Time based on the urgency with which the local county child<br>welfare agency needs to respond.<br>Intake caseworkers must have a current knowledge of statutory guidelines to identify and<br>categorize child abuse, neglect and/or dependency allegations. They must also be able to<br>interview a reporter in a way that elicits the reporter's concern. Intake caseworkers must<br>consider the child's age, developmental status, other child vulnerabilities, and current family<br>life events. The local county child welfare agency has the authority to intervene only when<br>the allegation, if true, would meet the legal definitions. The appropriate maltreatment must<br>be selected based on the maltreatment screening tools.<br>The first progress tab in the Screening and Response Tool is the Screening and Connecting<br>Maltreatments. There are three maltreatment allegation screening criteria: Abuse, Neglect<br>and Dependency. # Intake Decision Making

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**DETERMINATION OF COUNTY OR STATE ASSIGNMENT**<br>The intake decision determines if there is a valid Child Protective Services (CPS)<br>report, how quickly the agency needs to respond, and which local county child<br>welfare agency is responsible for conducting the CPS Assessment.<br>COUNTY ASSIGNMENT<br>The Intake caseworker and supervisor managing the CPS Intake must:<br>● Screen the report;<br>● Determine if the maltreatment alleged in the report is sufficient to warrant<br>intervention based on suspected abuse, neglect, and/or dependency;<br>● Determine how quickly the agency needs to respond;<br>● Determine which local county child welfare agency is responsible for<br>conducting the CPS Assessment; and<br>● Send the reporter notification letter.<br>● Send all other required notifications (i.e. Law Enforcement, DA, DCDEE, DHSR,<br>Military, RAMS)<br>When a reporter contacts a county where the child does not reside, the county<br>receiving the report must still take all the information from the reporter and make<br>verbal notification to the county where the child resides. The county where the child<br>resides must make a screening decision and, if accepted, determine the assessment<br>track and response time UNLESS the information provided in the report is urgent and<br>requires an immediate response by the agency receiving the report. Verbal<br>notification must also be made informing the county of the child's location and that<br>initiation has begun by the county receiving the report.<br>Additional requirements are contained in the "Conflict of Interest" section in the<br>Cross Function manual of the NC Child Welfare manual.|**DETERMINATION OF COUNTY OR STATE ASSIGNMENT**<br>The intake decision determines if there is a valid Child Protective Services (CPS)<br>report, how quickly the agency needs to respond, and which local county child<br>welfare agency is responsible for conducting the CPS Assessment.<br>COUNTY ASSIGNMENT<br>The Intake caseworker and supervisor managing the CPS Intake must:<br>● Screen the report;<br>● Determine if the maltreatment alleged in the report is sufficient to warrant<br>intervention based on suspected abuse, neglect, and/or dependency;<br>● Determine how quickly the agency needs to respond;<br>● Determine which local county child welfare agency is responsible for<br>conducting the CPS Assessment; and<br>● Send the reporter notification letter.<br>● Send all other required notifications (i.e. Law Enforcement, DA, DCDEE, DHSR,<br>Military, RAMS)<br>When a reporter contacts a county where the child does not reside, the county<br>receiving the report must still take all the information from the reporter and make<br>verbal notification to the county where the child resides. The county where the child<br>resides must make a screening decision and, if accepted, determine the assessment<br>track and response time UNLESS the information provided in the report is urgent and<br>requires an immediate response by the agency receiving the report. Verbal<br>notification must also be made informing the county of the child's location and that<br>initiation has begun by the county receiving the report.<br>Additional requirements are contained in the "Conflict of Interest" section in the<br>Cross Function manual of the NC Child Welfare manual.|**DETERMINATION OF COUNTY OR STATE ASSIGNMENT**<br>The intake decision determines if there is a valid Child Protective Services (CPS)<br>report, how quickly the agency needs to respond, and which local county child<br>welfare agency is responsible for conducting the CPS Assessment.<br>COUNTY ASSIGNMENT<br>The Intake caseworker and supervisor managing the CPS Intake must:<br>● Screen the report;<br>● Determine if the maltreatment alleged in the report is sufficient to warrant<br>intervention based on suspected abuse, neglect, and/or dependency;<br>● Determine how quickly the agency needs to respond;<br>● Determine which local county child welfare agency is responsible for<br>conducting the CPS Assessment; and<br>● Send the reporter notification letter.<br>● Send all other required notifications (i.e. Law Enforcement, DA, DCDEE, DHSR,<br>Military, RAMS)<br>When a reporter contacts a county where the child does not reside, the county<br>receiving the report must still take all the information from the reporter and make<br>verbal notification to the county where the child resides. The county where the child<br>resides must make a screening decision and, if accepted, determine the assessment<br>track and response time UNLESS the information provided in the report is urgent and<br>requires an immediate response by the agency receiving the report. Verbal<br>notification must also be made informing the county of the child's location and that<br>initiation has begun by the county receiving the report.<br>Additional requirements are contained in the "Conflict of Interest" section in the<br>Cross Function manual of the NC Child Welfare manual.|The Intake caseworker should gather the demographic and<br>narrative information, current location of the child as well as<br>any other information needed to make an accurate screening<br>decision. When the Intake caseworker determines there is a<br>conflict of interest or that the child does not reside in their<br>county, the Intake caseworker must send the information in<br>the system to the county that is responsible for assessing the<br>report to complete the Screening and Response Tool in the<br>system. The county with the conflict of interest must make<br>verbal notification and receive confirmation from the<br>receiving county so that a timely assessment can be initiated.<br>The Intake caseworker should not expect the caller to find<br>the correct number to call or make the call to another DSS<br>agency. The caller should be advised that their name and<br>information will be referred to another agency and that, if<br>necessary, this agency may call the reporter seeking more<br>information. The<br>timeframe for initiation begins with the first contact with the<br>reporter. The responsibility for ensuring that the transfer of<br>information occurred lies with the county screening the<br>report.|

# Intake Decision Making

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**DETERMINATION OF ASSESSMENT APPROACH AND RESPONSE PRIORITY**<br>After completing the Screening Criteria, if one of the maltreatment types is<br>selected, the Intake caseworker will be guided by the system to complete<br>the Assessment Track and Response Time. PATH NC is designed to support<br>consistency in the Response Time and Assessment Track by selecting based<br>on the maltreatment types and allegation characteristics chosen.<br>The Response Time and Track progress tab determines the time frame for<br>the response for all reports accepted for a CPS Assessment (Immediate—8<br>hours, within 24 hours, or within 72 hours) and track (Family Assessment or<br>Investigative Assessment). Intake caseworkers will be directed to the<br>appropriate response matrix, based on the allegation type.<br>The first matrix includes allegations of abuse and certain identified neglect<br>cases: child fatality, near fatality, human trafficking, abuse (physical, sexual,<br>emotional, moral), or abandonment.<br>The second matrix includes allegations of neglect and/or dependency<br>allegations only.<br>If the report includes allegations on both response matrices, the Intake<br>caseworker will complete the first matrix (abuse, abandonment, child<br>fatality, near fatality, human trafficking).<br>All reports that are screened "in" must be assigned for response according to<br>the allegation characteristics selected in the Response Time and Assessment<br>Track progress tab. This will be auto populated based on completion of the<br>tool.|**DETERMINATION OF ASSESSMENT APPROACH AND RESPONSE PRIORITY**<br>After completing the Screening Criteria, if one of the maltreatment types is<br>selected, the Intake caseworker will be guided by the system to complete<br>the Assessment Track and Response Time. This will be auto populated based on completion of the<br>tool.|**DETERMINATION OF ASSESSMENT APPROACH AND RESPONSE PRIORITY**<br>After completing the Screening Criteria, if one of the maltreatment types is<br>selected, the Intake caseworker will be guided by the system to complete<br>the Assessment Track and Response Time. PATH NC is designed to support<br>consistency in the Response Time and Assessment Track by selecting based<br>on the maltreatment types and allegation characteristics chosen.<br>The Response Time and Track progress tab determines the time frame for<br>the response for all reports accepted for a CPS Assessment (Immediate—8<br>hours, within 24 hours, or within 72 hours) and track (Family Assessment or<br>Investigative Assessment). | | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**DEFINITIONS OF FAMILY ASSESSMENT AND INVESTIGATIVE ASSESSMENT**<br>FAMILY ASSESSMENT RESPONSE<br>A response that is:<br>● Family-centered;<br>● Protection- and prevention-oriented; and<br>● Evaluates the strengths and needs of the juvenile's family, as well as the condition of the juvenile.<br>The Family Assessment response is appropriate for reports that contain allegations meeting the statutory<br>definitions of neglect and/or dependency and**does not** meet the following allegation characteristics:<br>•<br>Child exposed to methamphetamine lab/fentanyl;<br>•<br>Medical neglect of an infant with life-threatening condition;<br>•<br>Child is in custody of child welfare, family foster home or residential facility;<br>•<br>Physician or law enforcement has taken emergency custody;<br>•<br>A hospitalized child due to suspected neglect;<br>•<br> or OTHER reason described.<br>INVESTIGATIVE ASSESSMENT RESPONSE<br>A response to reports of child abuse and selected reports of child neglect and dependency that is a formal<br>information gathering process to determine whether a juvenile is abused, neglected, or dependent.<br>The Investigative Assessment response is appropriate when reports include an allegation of child fatality, near<br>fatality, human trafficking, abuse, or abandonment (physical, sexual, emotional, moral).<br>If response track for neglect or dependency includes any of the following, the response must be changed to<br>Investigative Assessment:<br>•<br>Child exposed to methamphetamine lab/fentanyl;<br>•<br>Medical neglect of an infant with life-threatening condition;<br>•<br>Child is in custody of child welfare, foster care or residential care;<br>•<br>Physician or law enforcement has taken emergency custody;<br>•<br> or OTHER reason described.|**DEFINITIONS OF FAMILY ASSESSMENT AND INVESTIGATIVE ASSESSMENT**<br>FAMILY ASSESSMENT RESPONSE<br>A response that is:<br>● Family-centered;<br>● Protection- and prevention-oriented; and<br>● Evaluates the strengths and needs of the juvenile's family, as well as the condition of the juvenile.<br>The Family Assessment response is appropriate for reports that contain allegations meeting the statutory<br>definitions of neglect and/or dependency and**does not** meet the following allegation characteristics:<br>•<br>Child exposed to methamphetamine lab/fentanyl;<br>•<br>Medical neglect of an infant with life-threatening condition;<br>•<br>Child is in custody of child welfare, family foster home or residential facility;<br>•<br>Physician or law enforcement has taken emergency custody;<br>•<br>A hospitalized child due to suspected neglect;<br>•<br> or OTHER reason described.<br>INVESTIGATIVE ASSESSMENT RESPONSE<br>A response to reports of child abuse and selected reports of child neglect and dependency that is a formal<br>information gathering process to determine whether a juvenile is abused, neglected, or dependent.<br>The Investigative Assessment response is appropriate when reports include an allegation of child fatality, near<br>fatality, human trafficking, abuse, or abandonment (physical, sexual, emotional, moral).<br>If response track for neglect or dependency includes any of the following, the response must be changed to<br>Investigative Assessment:<br>•<br>Child exposed to methamphetamine lab/fentanyl;<br>•<br>Medical neglect of an infant with life-threatening condition;<br>•<br>Child is in custody of child welfare, foster care or residential care;<br>•<br>Physician or law enforcement has taken emergency custody;<br>•<br> or OTHER reason described.|**DEFINITIONS OF FAMILY ASSESSMENT AND INVESTIGATIVE ASSESSMENT**<br>FAMILY ASSESSMENT RESPONSE<br>A response that is:<br>● Family-centered;<br>● Protection- and prevention-oriented; and<br>● Evaluates the strengths and needs of the juvenile's family, as well as the condition of the juvenile.<br>The Family Assessment response is appropriate for reports that contain allegations meeting the statutory<br>definitions of neglect and/or dependency and**does not** meet the following allegation characteristics:<br>•<br>Child exposed to methamphetamine lab/fentanyl;<br>•<br>Medical neglect of an infant with life-threatening condition;<br>•<br>Child is in custody of child welfare, family foster home or residential facility;<br>•<br>Physician or law enforcement has taken emergency custody;<br>•<br>A hospitalized child due to suspected neglect;<br>•<br> or OTHER reason described.<br>INVESTIGATIVE ASSESSMENT RESPONSE<br>A response to reports of child abuse and selected reports of child neglect and dependency that is a formal<br>information gathering process to determine whether a juvenile is abused, neglected, or dependent.<br>The Investigative Assessment response is appropriate when reports include an allegation of child fatality, near<br>fatality, human trafficking, abuse, or abandonment (physical, sexual, emotional, moral).<br>If response track for neglect or dependency includes any of the following, the response must be changed to<br>Investigative Assessment:<br>•<br>Child exposed to methamphetamine lab/fentanyl;<br>•<br>Medical neglect of an infant with life-threatening condition;<br>•<br>Child is in custody of child welfare, foster care or residential care;<br>•<br>Physician or law enforcement has taken emergency custody;<br>•<br> or OTHER reason described.|Family centered is not the same as<br>family friendly. The primary role of<br>Child Protective Services is to ensure<br>the safetyof children. Being family<br>centered requires caseworkers to<br>focus on solutions that allow<br>children to remain safely with their<br>families**BUT**does not negate the<br>statutory requirement to remove<br>children from unsafe situations<br>when there are no other options at<br>the expense of getting along with<br>the family.|Family centered is not the same as<br>family friendly. Being family<br>centered requires caseworkers to<br>focus on solutions that allow<br>children to remain safely with their<br>families**BUT**does not negate the<br>statutory requirement to remove<br>children from unsafe situations<br>when there are no other options at<br>the expense of getting along with<br>the family.|

<!-- page: 42 -->
# Intake Decision Making

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**ASSIGNMENT OF ASSESSMENT APPROACH**<br>Regardless of the assignment to either CPS Assessment response, when the local county child welfare agency<br>receives a valid CPS report, the agency must act to ensure the safety of the child through the provision of<br>protective services.<br>As the timeframe for responding to reports of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency begins at the time the<br>reporter contacts the local county child welfare agency, assignment of the report for assessment must occur<br>as soon as the Screening and Response tool is completed.|**ASSIGNMENT OF ASSESSMENT APPROACH**<br>Regardless of the assignment to either CPS Assessment response, when the local county child welfare agency<br>receives a valid CPS report, the agency must act to ensure the safety of the child through the provision of<br>protective services.<br>As the timeframe for responding to reports of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency begins at the time the<br>reporter contacts the local county child welfare agency, assignment of the report for assessment must occur<br>as soon as the Screening and Response tool is completed.|**ASSIGNMENT OF ASSESSMENT APPROACH**<br>Regardless of the assignment to either CPS Assessment response, when the local county child welfare agency<br>receives a valid CPS report, the agency must act to ensure the safety of the child through the provision of<br>protective services.<br>As the timeframe for responding to reports of abuse, neglect, and/or dependency begins at the time the<br>reporter contacts the local county child welfare agency, assignment of the report for assessment must occur<br>as soon as the Screening and Response tool is completed.||||

# Intake Decision Making

| | Protocol - What you must do | | | Guidance - How you should do it | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**TWO-LEVEL DECISION CONSULTATION**<br>Two-level decisions must occur on every CPS Intake completed. The<br>screening decision(s) must include a discussion between the Intake<br>caseworker and a supervisor (or other management position) about Step 1<br>and Step 2 of the Screening and Response Tool.<br>All persons participating in the screening decision must sign or approve the<br>Screening and Response Tool where indicated.|**TWO-LEVEL DECISION CONSULTATION**<br>Two-level decisions must occur on every CPS Intake completed. The<br>screening decision(s) must include a discussion between the Intake<br>caseworker and a supervisor (or other management position) about Step 1<br>and Step 2 of the Screening and Response Tool.<br>All persons participating in the screening decision must sign or approve the<br>Screening and Response Tool where indicated.|**TWO-LEVEL DECISION CONSULTATION**<br>Two-level decisions must occur on every CPS Intake completed. However, there may be<br>circumstances that require another Intake caseworker or another<br>supervisor or a higher-level manager in the agency to participate in a<br>review or the decision making.<br>When a supervisor does not have access to a higher-level manager for<br>a 2nd level review when screening an Intake report, the supervisor may<br>send to another supervisor for the 2nd level review.|Two-level decisions for CPS Intake reports should involve the assigned<br>caseworker and that worker's supervisor. However, there may be<br>circumstances that require another Intake caseworker or another<br>supervisor or a higher-level manager in the agency to participate in a<br>review or the decision making.<br>When a supervisor does not have access to a higher-level manager for<br>a 2nd level review when screening an Intake report, the supervisor may<br>send to another supervisor for the 2nd level review.|Two-level decisions for CPS Intake reports should involve the assigned<br>caseworker and that worker's supervisor. However, there may be<br>circumstances that require another Intake caseworker or another<br>supervisor or a higher-level manager in the agency to participate in a<br>review or the decision making.<br>When a supervisor does not have access to a higher-level manager for<br>a 2nd level review when screening an Intake report, the supervisor may<br>send to another supervisor for the 2nd level review.|

|**Felony child abuse (§14-318.4)**<br>•<br>Serious bodily injury(G.S. §14-318.4(d)(1) Bodily injury that creates a substantial risk<br>of death or that causes serious permanent disfigurement, coma, a permanent or<br>protracted condition that causes extreme pain, or permanent or protracted loss or<br>impairment of the function of any bodily member or organ, or that results in<br>prolonged hospitalization.<br>•<br>Serious physical injury (G.S. §14-318.4(d)(2) Physical injury that causes great pain<br>and suffering. The term includes serious mental injury.<br>•<br>Sexually Violent Offense(G.S. §14-208.6(5)) This also includes the following: an<br>attempt, solicitation, or conspiracy to commit any of these offenses; aiding and<br>abetting any of these offenses.<br>•<br>Violent Offense(G.S. 14-318.6(5)) any offense that inflicts upon the juvenile serious<br>bodily injury or serious physical injury by other than accidental means. This term<br>also includes the following: an attempt, solicitation, or conspiracy to commit any of<br>these offenses: aiding and abetting any of these offenses.<br>REPORTER NOTIFICATION<br>For all CPS Intake reports, there must be documentation that:<br>a) Written notice was sent to the person making the report within<br>5 working days after receipt of the report;<br>b) The person making the report waived their right to notice; or<br>c) The person making the report refused to provide identifying information.<br>The notice to the reporter must include:<br>a) A statement about whether the report was accepted for CPS Assessment based on<br>statutory definitions and citing the relevant statutes, and identify the type of CPS<br>Assessment that includes a brief description<br>b) The date the report was made;|**Felony child abuse (§14-318.4)**<br>•<br>Serious bodily injury(G.S. §14-318.4(d)(1) Bodily injury that creates a substantial risk<br>of death or that causes serious permanent disfigurement, coma, a permanent or<br>protracted condition that causes extreme pain, or permanent or protracted loss or<br>impairment of the function of any bodily member or organ, or that results in<br>prolonged hospitalization.<br>•<br>Serious physical injury (G.S. §14-318.4(d)(2) Physical injury that causes great pain<br>and suffering. This term<br>also includes the following: an attempt, solicitation, or conspiracy to commit any of<br>these offenses: aiding and abetting any of these offenses.<br>REPORTER NOTIFICATION<br>For all CPS Intake reports, there must be documentation that:<br>a) Written notice was sent to the person making the report within<br>5 working days after receipt of the report;<br>b) The person making the report waived their right to notice; or<br>c) The person making the report refused to provide identifying information.<br>The notice to the reporter must include:<br>a) A statement about whether the report was accepted for CPS Assessment based on<br>statutory definitions and citing the relevant statutes, and identify the type of CPS<br>Assessment that includes a brief description<br>b) The date the report was made;|The requirement for written notification does not<br>negate the local county child welfare agency's ability<br>to share the screening decision with the reporter<br>through other means prior to receipt of the 5-day<br>letter if the inquiry is an effort to provide protective<br>services to the family. Examples of such situations<br>include a hospital social worker wanting to know the<br>screening decision prior to a child's discharge, or a<br>police officer who is waiting for an initiating<br>caseworker to arrive on the scene.<br>Reference G.S. 7B-309 for specific information about<br>the immunity ofpersons reporting and cooperating in|The requirement for written notification does not<br>negate the local county child welfare agency's ability<br>to share the screening decision with the reporter<br>through other means prior to receipt of the 5-day<br>letter if the inquiry is an effort to provide protective<br>services to the family. Examples of such situations<br>include a hospital social worker wanting to know the<br>screening decision prior to a child's discharge, or a<br>police officer who is waiting for an initiating<br>caseworker to arrive on the scene.<br>Reference G.S. 7B-309 for specific information about<br>the immunity ofpersons reporting and cooperating in|The requirement for written notification does not<br>negate the local county child welfare agency's ability<br>to share the screening decision with the reporter<br>through other means prior to receipt of the 5-day<br>letter if the inquiry is an effort to provide protective<br>services to the family. Examples of such situations<br>include a hospital social worker wanting to know the<br>screening decision prior to a child's discharge, or a<br>police officer who is waiting for an initiating<br>caseworker to arrive on the scene.<br>Reference G.S. 7B-309 for specific information about<br>the immunity ofpersons reporting and cooperating in|

|**MALTREATMENT TYPE**<br>Each maltreatment type is listed below and includes when a report must<br>be accepted. Any subtypes that match the narrative information<br>provided by the reporter must be checked in the system.<br>**ABUSE**<br>**Human Trafficking**<br>A report regarding a child being directly or indirectly given, promised, or<br>received in exchange for anything regardless of whether the child is used<br>for labor or sex, or to satisfy a debt must be screened for assessment. All<br>reports with allegations of human trafficking must be screened for<br>assessment regardless of the perpetrator's status as a caretaker. This can also include<br>children working long hours for little to no pay and working in places or<br>engaging in activities that are illegal for children to perform.<br>Child Trafficking: A child exchanged for anything or as settlement for a<br>debt.
