# Maltreatment Types

Attachment A

|Physical Abuse<br>Serious Physical Injury<br>Any report received where the allegations are serious physical injury<br>inflicted or allowed to be inflicted on a child by a caretaker or a<br>caretaker allowing others to inflict serious physical injury must be<br>accepted for assessment. Serious physical injury can be an intense, acute<br>injury OR characterized by the child suffering lasting pain. Injuries may<br>be current or in different stages of healing. Serious physical injury<br>examples include:<br>• Bruising, lacerations, welts, bites, or swelling on the head, face, eyes,<br>throat, chest, belly/abdomen, genitalia, or other sensitive and<br>vulnerable areas of the body;<br>• Extensive bruising, welts, bites, or swelling and any lacerations on<br>legs, arms, back, or buttocks;<br>• Fractured/broken bones;<br>• Injuries requiring medical attention, even if medical attention has<br>not been sought; and<br>• Patterns of burns consistent with maltreatment (e.g., cigarette<br>burns, immersion burns).<br>The injuries listed above are not intended to be an all-inclusive list but<br>are an indication of information that does warrant an Investigative<br>Assessment. Any allegations that result in the injuries listed above<br>regardless of the caretakers' intent must be screened in as Abuse under<br>Severe Physical Injury.<br>Unexplained Physical Injury<br>Allegations received that include a serious physical injury to a child,<br>consistent with the definition above, that is not explained, or the<br>explanation is not plausible or consistent with the injury, or the<br>perpetrator is unknown must be accepted for assessment under this<br>item. This item must also be selected when any injury to a non-cruising<br>child that is not explained, or the explanation is not plausible or|Serious Physical Injury<br>Serious Physical Injury includes corporal punishment and injuries to the<br>child as a result of domestic violence. There may be circumstances where a<br>child has bruises that do not rise to the level of abuse but are considered<br>unsafe discipline (refer to the Unsafe Discipline Maltreatment Screening<br>Tool), as well as situations where there may be bruising and there is no<br>abuse or neglect. Gathering all the information the reporter has will be<br>important to making the correct screening decision. For reports of<br>discipline that do not result in serious physical injury and that have<br>harmed the child or present a risk of harm, including reports of children<br>three or younger subject to corporal punishment, review Physical abuse -<br>Excessive or cruel punishment and Neglect - Unsafe discipline.<br>Unexplained Physical Injury<br>These reports may come from a healthcare provider stating that the injury<br>is consistent with non-accidental injuries, or an injury observed by the<br>reporter where the child and caretaker are unable to provide an<br>explanation for how the injury occurred. It is also important to consider<br>the child's age, developmental and activity level.|
|---|---|

# Maltreatment Types

Attachment A

|consistent with the injury is reported no matter how minor. This is called<br>a Sentinel injury.<br>A Sentinel injury is a visible, poorly explained small injury such as a<br>bruise on any part of the body or intraoral (mouth) injury in a pre-<br>cruising child often from abuse and can precede more serious abuse.<br>"Cruising" means the child can pull to a stand and take a few steps<br>holding onto something. Children typically learn to do this between 8<br>and 11 months of age. A child with a small bruise from abuse may have<br>severe internal injuries, so additional medical screening is necessary.<br>Excessive or Cruel Punishment<br>This includes any child who is three years old or younger, non-verbal, or<br>limited by developmental, behavioral, or physical disabilities with any<br>marks or injuries as a result of physical discipline/corporal punishment.<br>Use of any of the following discipline must be accepted for assessment:<br>• Actions that cause injury or pain<br>• Forced ingestion of non-food items or dangerous amounts of food<br>or water<br>• Use of ropes, duct tape, blankets or other restraints that restricts<br>the child's movement. This does not include placing a child in a<br>room or chair for a short period.<br>• Forced actions of the child such as extensive running, lifting, or<br>physical chores that are beyond the child's reasonable ability<br>• Actively and intentionally withholding or restricting access to basic<br>needs such as food, clean drinking water, clothing, shelter, toilet<br>and hygiene facilities to the extent that the child endures pain,<br>illness, or injury.<br>Substantial Risk of Physical Harm<br>When a report is received with allegations that the caretaker is engaging<br>in dangerous behavior toward or near the child and the behavior could<br>cause serious physical injury the report must be accepted for<br>assessment. This includes:|Examples of Sentinel injuries include:<br>• Bruises, regardless of size or color of bruise, anywhere on the body<br>• Injuries inside and outside of the mouth (e.g., bruising to tongue, cuts<br>in mouth)<br>• Broken blood vessels or red spots in the eyes (e.g., subconjunctival<br>hemorrhages)<br>Substantial risk of physical harm should also be considered in situations<br>where caretakers are providing drugs or alcohol to children. The Intake<br>caseworker should consider the age and what substance was given to the<br>child as well the outcome of providing the substance to the child.|
|---|---|

This<br>does not require a criminal charge or an accident to have occurred<br>for acceptance of a new report. This also includes<br>situations when the caretaker knows that the environment has a<br>substantial risk for serious non-accidental abuse or injury and allows the<br>child to remain in the environment.<br>Sexual Abuse<br>Sexual Act on a Child by an Adult Caretaker or Other Adult in the<br>Household<br>Sexual abuse includes rape, intercourse, sodomy, fondling, oral sex,<br>incest, or sexual penetration-digital, penile, or foreign objects. Any<br>report that includes a child's disclosure, medical evidence, and/or a<br>credible witnessed act of sexual contact by a caretaker or other adult in<br>the home on a child must be accepted. # Maltreatment Types

Attachment A

| • Grooming: verbal, written, or physical behavior not overtly sexual<br>but likely designed to prepare a child for future sexual abuse. This<br>could look like a deliberate and escalating pattern of actions taken<br>to lower a child's inhibitions (e.g., treating the child as "more<br>special" than other children, talking about sexual topics that are age<br>inappropriate, commenting on physical traits/changes of the child,<br>escalating touching from innocent to inappropriate, and<br>"accidental" self-exposure by the adult).<br>• A caretaker who views or possesses child pornography.<br>Emotional Abuse, Mental Injury<br>Reports must be accepted when there are allegations that the<br>caretaker's action or inaction has led to a child's severe anxiety,<br>depression, withdrawal, or aggressive behavior towards themselves or<br>others. There may be single incidents or a pattern of behavior that<br>would be of lesser concern if it was a single incident. Examples include,<br>but are not limited to:<br>• A caretaker who rejects or refuses to acknowledge the child's worth<br>and the legitimacy of the child's needs by singling out one child to<br>criticize or punish, belittling the child, or shaming the child.<br>• A caretaker who threatens the child with being sent away or tells<br>the child he/she is unwanted.<br>• A caretaker who treats the child differently from other children in<br>the home, does not allow the child the same food as the rest of the<br>family and withholds other provisions provided to the rest of the<br>household, and/or physically separates the child from the family.<br>• A caretaker who publicly humiliates the child in the guise of<br>discipline (e.g., making an older child wear a diaper for bedwetting,<br>forcing the child to wear soiled clothes to school, removing the<br>child's hair). | |
|---|---|

# Maltreatment Types

|Emotional abuse also includes a caretaker terrorizing and/or creating a<br>climate of fear by verbally assaulting or bullying the child with the<br>intention of causing the child to experience extreme fear. Actions could<br>include but are not limited to:<br>• A caretaker who harms and/or threatens harm to self, child, or<br>loved ones, including pets.<br>• A caretaker who harms animals in front of the child and/or places<br>the child in dangerous situations.<br>• A caretaker who makes credible threats or there are previous<br>situations and/or examples of the caretaker carrying out acts that<br>produce fear in the child that it will happen again.<br>• A child who is experiencing reasonable fears of retribution or<br>retaliation from the caretaker.<br>• A child's fearful response escalates at the mention of home, people,<br>and/or circumstances associated with reported incidents.<br>When a caretaker uses isolation and ignoring in an extreme manner that<br>is to the detriment of the child, it must be assessed for emotional abuse.<br>This would be a caretaker who limits interactions, deprives the child of<br>essential stimulation and responses, or cuts the child off from normal<br>opportunities for social or cultural interaction, preventing the child from<br>forming familial bonds or friendships and making the child believe that<br>he/she is alone in the world. These things<br>should be considered in relation to the child's age, developmental<br>ability, and mental health. This consideration should also include the<br>location of the child and the length of time alone and the time of day.<br>When the babysitter or older sibling providing supervision is a minor<br>their age, developmental ability, and mental health should also be<br>considered in regard to whether they are capable of providing care to<br>others. If a caretaker is aware of a child's previous behaviors that<br>indicated that they needed a more structured supervision plan and the<br>caretaker failed to put one in place, a report should be considered<br>based on the safety of that child with the past behaviors.<br>It is important to get sufficient information at Intake regarding the<br>behavior of the parent(s), as well as the behavior of the minor<br>child(ren). When the parent has no knowledge of the child's sexual<br>activity, the child's age, behaviors, and developmental level as well as<br>any power and control dynamics between the children impact whether<br>a CPS Assessment should be accepted.<br>In situations where a caretaker is consuming alcohol/drugs with the<br>child, unsafe supervision should be considered as the parent may not<br>be providing adequate supervision.<br>**Unsafe Discipline**|

|**Parent, Guardian, or Custodian Has Refused to Follow the**<br>**Recommendations of the Juvenile and Family Team**<br>Any reports where the parent, guardian, or custodian is refusing to follow the<br>recommendations of the Juvenile and Family Team,**AND**this refusal causes<br>abuse, neglect and/or dependency or puts the juvenile at substantial risk of<br>harm must be accepted for assessment. Neglect**<br>Physical Health<br>If the child has exceptional needs, such as being medically fragile, which<br>the parent/caretaker does not or cannot meet, a CPS Assessment may<br>be warranted. # Maltreatment Types

Attachment A

| | Protocol-What you must do | | | Guidance-What you should do | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|Any caretaker that fails to ensure that their school-age child (ages seven<br>through 15, or age of enrollment if younger than seven) is provided with an<br>education must be assessed for educational neglect.<br>Some additional examples of allegations that must be accepted include:<br>•<br>A child between the ages of seven through 15 (school-age child) who<br>is not enrolled in school AND is not registered in a homeschooling<br>program with the Division of Non-Public Education.<br>•<br>A reporting source that is not school personnel and has no details<br>about the school's action to engage the caretaker with concerns that<br>a school-age child has excessive unexcused absences during the<br>academic year that are negatively impacting the child.<br>•<br>A reporting source that is not school personnel and has no details<br>about the school's action to engage the caretaker with concerns that<br>a school-age child is not receiving a sanctioned education.<br>•<br>A caretaker who refuses to allow or fails to obtain recommended<br>special education or remedial education services for a school-age<br>child.<br>•<br>A school-age child who has excessive unexcused absences this<br>academic year, and despite formal intervention by the school, the<br>school reports that the caretaker has been unable to be contacted,<br>has been uncooperative with school officials, or cannot provide an<br>appropriate explanation for the child's absences. # Maltreatment Types

Attachment A

| | Protocol-What you must do | | | Guidance-What you should do | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**ABANDONMENT**<br>A report must be accepted when the reporter alleges that the parent is not willing<br>to provide care for the child and the non-legal caretaker no longer wishes or is no<br>longer able to continue. This includes the parent/caretaker:<br>•<br>Establishing a new residence without the child.<br>•<br>Leaving the child longer than the planned time without communication or<br>support for the child<br>•<br>Has deserted the child without a plan for return.<br>•<br>Refuses to accept the child back home AND has not participated in finding<br>an alternative placement.<br>•<br>Removed the child from the home and/or refuses access and has not<br>provided a suitable alternative.<br>NCGS § 7B-302(a) requires workers to send a request to law enforcement to<br>investigate through the North Carolina Center for Missing Persons and other<br>National and State resources to determine whether the child has been reported<br>missing when the report alleges abandonment.|**ABANDONMENT**<br>A report must be accepted when the reporter alleges that the parent is not willing<br>to provide care for the child and the non-legal caretaker no longer wishes or is no<br>longer able to continue. This includes the parent/caretaker:<br>•<br>Establishing a new residence without the child.<br>•<br>Leaving the child longer than the planned time without communication or<br>support for the child<br>•<br>Has deserted the child without a plan for return.<br>•<br>Refuses to accept the child back home AND has not participated in finding<br>an alternative placement.<br>•<br>Removed the child from the home and/or refuses access and has not<br>provided a suitable alternative.<br>NCGS § 7B-302(a) requires workers to send a request to law enforcement to<br>investigate through the North Carolina Center for Missing Persons and other<br>National and State resources to determine whether the child has been reported<br>missing when the report alleges abandonment.|**ABANDONMENT**<br>A report must be accepted when the reporter alleges that the parent is not willing<br>to provide care for the child and the non-legal caretaker no longer wishes or is no<br>longer able to continue. This includes the parent/caretaker:<br>•<br>Establishing a new residence without the child.<br>•<br>Leaving the child longer than the planned time without communication or<br>support for the child<br>•<br>Has deserted the child without a plan for return.<br>•<br>Refuses to accept the child back home AND has not participated in finding<br>an alternative placement.<br>•<br>Removed the child from the home and/or refuses access and has not<br>provided a suitable alternative.<br>NCGS § 7B-302(a) requires workers to send a request to law enforcement to<br>investigate through the North Carolina Center for Missing Persons and other<br>National and State resources to determine whether the child has been reported<br>missing when the report alleges abandonment.|**ABANDONMENT**<br>Abandonment is a conscious decision made by the parent/caretaker<br>to leave the child and a clear demonstration that the<br>parent/caretaker does not intend to resume parental responsibilities<br>for the child. In reports where there are no safety concerns and the<br>person providing care is seeking a legal arrangement to continue<br>providing care for the child, they should be directed to the appropriate<br>county resources rather than accepting it for assessment. It may be<br>necessary for the Intake caseworker to consult with their legal counsel for<br>the appropriate county resources.<br>In cases where there are no safety issues and the person providing care is<br>having trouble enrolling the child in school, the Intake caseworker should<br>refer them to NCGS § 115C-366.<br>The Intake caseworker should elicit additional information in cases<br>involving a mental health or medical facility who is reporting that a<br>caretaker is refusing to come and get a child. If the caretaker is unable to<br>provide care for the child safely in the home, then a dependency<br>screening decision should be reviewed.<br>Informal caretakers are placements made by the parent, custodian or<br>legal guardian outside of child welfare input. In cases where informal<br>caretakers who do not have a legal arrangement are seeking<br>assistance in achieving a legal arrangement; and there are no safety<br>concern, should be directed to appropriate county resources rather<br>than screening in the referral for abandonment. A Intake caseworker<br>should consult with their legal counsel on the appropriate county<br>resources to assist the informal caretaker with accessing legal<br>custody.|**ABANDONMENT**<br>Abandonment is a conscious decision made by the parent/caretaker<br>to leave the child and a clear demonstration that the<br>parent/caretaker does not intend to resume parental responsibilities<br>for the child. In reports where there are no safety concerns and the<br>person providing care is seeking a legal arrangement to continue<br>providing care for the child, they should be directed to the appropriate<br>county resources rather than accepting it for assessment. It may be<br>necessary for the Intake caseworker to consult with their legal counsel for<br>the appropriate county resources.<br>In cases where there are no safety issues and the person providing care is<br>having trouble enrolling the child in school, the Intake caseworker should<br>refer them to NCGS § 115C-366.<br>The Intake caseworker should elicit additional information in cases<br>involving a mental health or medical facility who is reporting that a<br>caretaker is refusing to come and get a child. If the caretaker is unable to<br>provide care for the child safely in the home, then a dependency<br>screening decision should be reviewed.<br>Informal caretakers are placements made by the parent, custodian or<br>legal guardian outside of child welfare input. In cases where informal<br>caretakers who do not have a legal arrangement are seeking<br>assistance in achieving a legal arrangement; and there are no safety<br>concern, should be directed to appropriate county resources rather<br>than screening in the referral for abandonment. A Intake caseworker<br>should consult with their legal counsel on the appropriate county<br>resources to assist the informal caretaker with accessing legal<br>custody.||

<!-- page: 68 -->
# Maltreatment Types

Attachment A

| | Protocol-What you must do | | | Guidance-What you should do | |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
|**DEPENDENCY**<br>When a caretaker is unable to care for a child as a result of incarceration,<br>hospitalization, physical or mental incapacity AND there is no safe alternative a<br>report must be accepted. Dependency also includes when a caretaker's ability is<br>substantially impaired to the extent that they are unable to meet the child's basic<br>needs. This could be due to a prescribed medication, physical illness, or emotional<br>state that affects alertness, availability, and decision making. A report for<br>dependency should be accepted in cases where a child is being discharged from a<br>hospital or facility and the caretaker has communicated that they are unable to<br>meet the needs for the child safely in the home AND has participated in<br>attempting to find an appropriate placement.<br>**SAFE SURRENDER INFANT**<br>A report for dependency must be accepted for all infants less than 30 days old<br>who are safely surrendered by a parent voluntarily delivering the infant to one of<br>the safe persons listed below and does not express an intent to return for the<br>infant.<br>•<br>A health care provider, as defined under G.S 90-21.11, who is on duty or<br>at a hospital or at a local or district health department or at a nonprofit<br>community health center.<br>•<br>A first responder who is on duty, including a law enforcement officer, a<br>certified emergency medical services worker, or a firefighter.<br>•<br>A social services worker who is on duty or at a local department of social<br>services<br>If, after the infant has been safely surrendered, the parent returns for the child the<br>report must be accepted for a family assessment to ensure the safety of the<br>home.|**DEPENDENCY**<br>When a caretaker is unable to care for a child as a result of incarceration,<br>hospitalization, physical or mental incapacity AND there is no safe alternative a<br>report must be accepted. Dependency also includes when a caretaker's ability is<br>substantially impaired to the extent that they are unable to meet the child's basic<br>needs. This could be due to a prescribed medication, physical illness, or emotional<br>state that affects alertness, availability, and decision making. A report for<br>dependency should be accepted in cases where a child is being discharged from a<br>hospital or facility and the caretaker has communicated that they are unable to<br>meet the needs for the child safely in the home AND has participated in<br>attempting to find an appropriate placement.<br>**SAFE SURRENDER INFANT**<br>A report for dependency must be accepted for all infants less than 30 days old<br>who are safely surrendered by a parent voluntarily delivering the infant to one of<br>the safe persons listed below and does not express an intent to return for the<br>infant.<br>•<br>A health care provider, as defined under G.S 90-21.11, who is on duty or<br>at a hospital or at a local or district health department or at a nonprofit<br>community health center.<br>•<br>A first responder who is on duty, including a law enforcement officer, a<br>certified emergency medical services worker, or a firefighter.<br>•<br>A social services worker who is on duty or at a local department of social<br>services<br>If, after the infant has been safely surrendered, the parent returns for the child the<br>report must be accepted for a family assessment to ensure the safety of the<br>home.|**DEPENDENCY**<br>When a caretaker is unable to care for a child as a result of incarceration,<br>hospitalization, physical or mental incapacity AND there is no safe alternative a<br>report must be accepted. Dependency also includes when a caretaker's ability is<br>substantially impaired to the extent that they are unable to meet the child's basic<br>needs. This could be due to a prescribed medication, physical illness, or emotional<br>state that affects alertness, availability, and decision making. A report for<br>dependency should be accepted in cases where a child is being discharged from a<br>hospital or facility and the caretaker has communicated that they are unable to<br>meet the needs for the child safely in the home AND has participated in<br>attempting to find an appropriate placement.<br>**SAFE SURRENDER INFANT**<br>A report for dependency must be accepted for all infants less than 30 days old<br>who are safely surrendered by a parent voluntarily delivering the infant to one of<br>the safe persons listed below and does not express an intent to return for the<br>infant.<br>•<br>A health care provider, as defined under G.S 90-21.11, who is on duty or<br>at a hospital or at a local or district health department or at a nonprofit<br>community health center.<br>•<br>A first responder who is on duty, including a law enforcement officer, a<br>certified emergency medical services worker, or a firefighter.<br>•<br>A social services worker who is on duty or at a local department of social<br>services<br>If, after the infant has been safely surrendered, the parent returns for the child the<br>report must be accepted for a family assessment to ensure the safety of the<br>home.|**DEPENDENCY**<br>If the caretaker has made a care plan for the child with a safe adult or<br>is otherwise able to safely mitigate the impact of their incapacity on<br>the child, this item should not be selected.<br>**SAFE SURRENDER INFANT**<br>It is important to note that in North Carolina the infant must be safely<br>surrendered to a person. While there are instances listed in statute that<br>identify places, the requirement is that the child be given to one of the<br>identified "safe" persons.<br>NCGS 7B-522 states that any of the safe persons listed who takes an<br>infant into temporary custody must perform any act necessary to protect<br>the physical health and well-being of the infant and immediately notify<br>the department of social services in the county where the infant is<br>surrendered. <!-- page: 70 -->
## Response Time and Track

Attachment B

The purpose of the response matrix is to provide structure for the determination of how quickly the CPS Assessment must be initiated. The response matrix will be introduced in the CWS for all reports accepted for assessment. Intake caseworkers will be directed to the appropriate response matrix, based on allegation type. If the report includes allegations on both response matrices, the Intake caseworker will complete Matrix A (Child Fatality, Near Fatality, Human Trafficking, Abuse, and Abandonment Allegations) before moving to Matrix B if additional allegations exist. The Intake caseworker should select all conditions that apply.

Consider child's age and developmental status, allegation severity, access of alleged perpetrator, and presence in the response criteria. After choosing the correct criteria the matrix will provide the appropriate response time (immediate—8 hours, within 24-hours, or within 72-hours). For an immediate response, initiation must occur at once, immediately after completion of the Intake report. The response should never exceed 24 hours for a report alleging physical or sexual abuse. The response should never exceed 72 hours for a report alleging neglect or dependency.

The responsibility to ensure safety begins at Intake. If the local county child welfare agency selects an initiation timeframe other than what the corresponding Response Matrix indicates, the decision must be documented and approved by a supervisor or higher management level. A report response time may be changed to initiate sooner, but CWS will not allow a report to be initiated at a later time.

|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Children express fear<br>through different, sometimes contradictory, behaviors. These may<br>include:<br>• Kicking, screaming, biting, spitting, throwing things;<br>• Shaking, quivering, crying uncontrollably;<br>• Running away, hiding, trying to escape the predicted<br>dismissal or departure time;<br>• Zoning out, emotionally distancing from others;<br>• Hypervigilance/exaggerated response to doors opening,<br>phones ringing, cars approaching;<br>• Physically distancing self from others. Finding a space (under<br>table, desk, bed where visual and auditory input are<br>decreased) and avoiding being touched or making eye<br>contact;<br>• Covering ears, closing eyes, and tucking in arms and legs as<br>much as possible; and<br>• Seeking protection behind an adult, under the adult's desk,<br>or in the corner of an adult's office/home.|
